<div class="ltx_page_main">

<div class="ltx_page_content">

# Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses

<div class="ltx_authors">

<span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Yoonho Lee  
Stanford &Roshen Nair  
Stanford &Qizheng Zhang  
Stanford &Kangwook Lee  
KRAFTON </span></span>
<span class="ltx_author_before">  </span><span class="ltx_creator ltx_role_author">
<span class="ltx_personname">Omar Khattab  
MIT &Chelsea Finn  
Stanford </span></span>

</div>

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

The performance of large language model (LLM) systems depends not only
on model weights, but also on their
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">harness</span>: the code that
determines what information to store, retrieve, and present to the
model. Yet harnesses are still designed largely by hand, and existing
text optimizers are poorly matched to this setting because they compress
feedback too aggressively: they are memoryless, condition only on scalar
scores, or restrict feedback to short templates or summaries. We
introduce <span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness</span>, an
outer-loop system that searches over harness code for LLM applications.
It uses an agentic proposer that accesses the source code, scores, and
execution traces of all prior candidates through a filesystem. On online
text classification, Meta-Harness improves over a state-of-the-art
context management system by 7.7 points while using 4$`\times`$ fewer
context tokens. On retrieval-augmented math reasoning, a single
discovered harness improves accuracy on 200 IMO-level problems by 4.7
points on average across five held-out models. On agentic coding,
discovered harnesses surpass the best hand-engineered baselines on
TerminalBench-2. Together, these results show that richer access to
prior experience can enable automated harness engineering.

</div>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Changing the harness around a fixed large language model (LLM) can
produce a 6$`\times`$ performance gap on the same
benchmark \[<a href="#bib.bib71" class="ltx_ref"
title="SWE-bench mobile: can large language model agents develop industry-level mobile applications?">46</a>\].
The <span id="S1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">harness</span>—the code that determines
what to store, retrieve, and show to the model—often matters as much as
the model itself. This sensitivity has led to growing interest in
<span id="S1.p1.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">harness
engineering</span>, the practice of refining the code around an LLM to
improve the overall system’s
performance \[<a href="#bib.bib50" class="ltx_ref"
title="Harness engineering: leveraging Codex in an agent-first world">35</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib80" class="ltx_ref"
title="Effective harnesses for long-running agents">20</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="I improved 15 LLMs at coding in one afternoon. only the harness changed.">9</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref" title="Harness engineering">8</a>\].
But despite its importance, harness engineering remains largely manual:
practitioners inspect failures, adjust heuristics, and iterate on a
small number of designs. In this paper, we ask whether this process
itself can be automated.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

A natural starting point is recent work on text optimization, since
harness engineering also involves iteratively improving text and code
artifacts using feedback from prior
attempts \[<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automatic prompt optimization with “gradient descent” and beam search">37</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref"
title="Mathematical discoveries from program search with large language models">38</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">34</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Feedback descent: open-ended text optimization via pairwise comparison">25</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>\].
However, these methods are poorly matched to harness engineering because
they typically operate with short-horizon or heavily compressed
feedback: some condition only on the current
candidate \[<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-refine: iterative refinement with self-feedback">30</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref"
title="Large language models as optimizers">50</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref"
title="TextGrad: automatic ”differentiation” via text">52</a>\], others
rely primarily on scalar scores \[<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">34</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="AdaEvolve: adaptive llm driven zeroth-order optimization">11</a>\],
and others restrict feedback to short templates or LLM-generated
summaries \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Feedback descent: open-ended text optimization via pairwise comparison">25</a>\].
This is a pragmatic scalability choice, not evidence that longer-range
dependencies are uninformative. Harnesses act over long horizons: a
single choice about what to store, when to retrieve it, or how to
present it can affect behavior many reasoning steps later. Compressed
feedback often removes the information needed to trace downstream
failures to earlier harness decisions. Across the tasks studied by
several representative text optimizers, the available context per
optimization step ranges from only 100 to 30,000
tokens (<a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>), far below the diagnostic
footprint of harness search. More broadly, work on retrieval and
memory-augmented language models suggests that useful context should
often be accessed adaptively rather than monolithically packed into a
single prompt \[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-intensive nlp tasks">27</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib92" class="ltx_ref"
title="Interleaving retrieval with chain-of-thought reasoning for knowledge-intensive multi-step questions">47</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemGPT: towards llms as operating systems.">36</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref"
title="Recursive language models">55</a>\].

</div>

<figure id="S1.F2" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2603.28052/assets/x3.png" id="S1.F2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="780"
height="278" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 2: </span> <span
id="S1.F2.5.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness search
loop.</span> <span id="S1.F2.6.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1)</span> An agent reads a filesystem
containing all prior candidates’ source code, execution traces, and
scores, and proposes a new harness. <span id="S1.F2.7.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2)</span> We evaluate the proposed
harness on evaluation tasks. <span id="S1.F2.8.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(3)</span> All logs (proposed code,
reasoning traces, evaluation scores) are stored in the filesystem in a
new directory, and the loop repeats.</figcaption>
</figure>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 1: </span> <span
id="S1.T1.11.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comparison of text
optimization methods and their settings.</span> Each row represents a
method collapsed across tasks. <span id="S1.T1.12.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Mtok/iter</span> is our best
estimate of the full context generated from one evaluation of a text
artifact in the <span id="S1.T1.13.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">largest setting considered in each
paper</span>. This paper considers settings that yield
orders-of-magnitude more context per artifact evaluation.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We address this limitation with <span id="S1.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness</span>, an agentic harness
for optimizing harnesses via end-to-end
search (<a href="#S1.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a>). Its proposer is a coding
agent, i.e., a language-model-based system that can invoke developer
tools and modify code. The choice of coding agent (rather than raw LLM)
matters because the amount of experience quickly exceeds context limits,
so the proposer must decide <span id="S1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">what</span> to inspect and validate
edits through direct interaction with the codebase. Its key design
choice is to expose <span id="S1.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">full history</span> through a
<span id="S1.p3.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">filesystem</span>,
enabling selective diagnosis of raw prior code and execution traces
rather than optimization from compressed per-candidate summaries. For
every previous candidate harness, the filesystem stores the source code,
evaluation scores, and execution traces, which the proposer retrieves
via standard operations such as <span id="S1.p3.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">grep</span> and
<span id="S1.p3.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">cat</span>
rather than ingesting them as a single prompt. In practice, the proposer
reads a median of <span id="S1.p3.1.7" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">82
files per iteration</span> in our most demanding setting, referencing
over 20 prior candidates per step (<a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>). In the settings we study, a
single evaluation can produce up to 10,000,000 tokens of diagnostic
information, roughly three orders of magnitude beyond the largest
feedback budgets used in prior text optimization
settings (<a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>).

</div>

<div id="S1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We evaluate Meta-Harness on online text classification, mathematical
reasoning, and agentic coding. On online text classification, harnesses
discovered by Meta-Harness improve over Agentic Context Engineering
(ACE, Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
\[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agentic context engineering: evolving contexts for self-improving language models">58</a>\])
by <span id="S1.p4.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">7.7 points</span>
while using 4$`\times`$ fewer context tokens, and match the next-best
text optimizer’s final performance after $`60`$ proposals with only
four (<a href="#S0.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>). On retrieval-augmented math
reasoning, a single discovered harness improves accuracy on 200
IMO-level problems by <span id="S1.p4.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">4.7 points</span> on average across five
held-out models. On TerminalBench-2, the discovered harness
<span id="S1.p4.2.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">surpasses
Terminus-KIRA and ranks \#1 among all Haiku 4.5 agents</span>.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Work

<div id="S2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

At a high level, Meta-Harness brings ideas from the broader literature
on credit assignment and
meta-learning \[<a href="#bib.bib57" class="ltx_ref"
title="A neural network that embeds its own meta-levels">39</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib70" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to learn: introduction and overview">45</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to learn by gradient descent by gradient descent">2</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="Model-agnostic meta-learning for fast adaptation of deep networks">16</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib61" class="ltx_ref"
title="Prototypical networks for few-shot learning">43</a>;
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_missing_citation ltx_ref_self">akyürek2023learningalgorithmincontextlearning</span>\]
in a new regime enabled by recent advances in coding agents. Rather than
updating model weights, the system assigns credit at the harness level:
it uses experience from past rollouts to deliberately reason about which
steps and components are responsible for failures, then rewrites the
external code that governs future behavior. More specifically, the
method lies at the intersection of several recent research threads; it
is most directly related to work on adaptive access to external context,
executable code search, and text optimization.

</div>

<div id="S2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">External memory and
adaptive access.</span> Several prior works note the benefits of
treating large knowledge sources or long inputs as external resources
that a language model accesses adaptively, rather than consuming them in
a single pass. Specifically, retrieval-augmented
generation \[<a href="#bib.bib41" class="ltx_ref"
title="Retrieval-augmented generation for knowledge-intensive nlp tasks">27</a>\],
interleaved retrieval and
reasoning \[<a href="#bib.bib92" class="ltx_ref"
title="Interleaving retrieval with chain-of-thought reasoning for knowledge-intensive multi-step questions">47</a>\],
memory-based agents \[<a href="#bib.bib52" class="ltx_ref"
title="MemGPT: towards llms as operating systems.">36</a>\], or
recursive language models \[<a href="#bib.bib89" class="ltx_ref"
title="Recursive language models">55</a>\] are mechanisms for adaptive
access to external context. Meta-Harness uses a similar access pattern,
but in the more demanding setting of harness engineering, where the
proposer selectively inspects a large external history of code, scores,
and execution traces to improve context-management procedures
themselves.

</div>

<div id="S2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Executable code
search.</span> Recent methods search over executable code for functions,
workflows, or agent designs. Early work proposes using large models as
mutation and crossover operators in evolutionary program
search \[<a href="#bib.bib96" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evolution through large models">26</a>\]. Later methods evolve
designated functions within fixed program
scaffolds \[<a href="#bib.bib56" class="ltx_ref"
title="Mathematical discoveries from program search with large language models">38</a>\],
use meta-agents to program new agents from prior
discoveries \[<a href="#bib.bib34" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automated design of agentic systems">19</a>\], or search over
workflow graphs for agentic
systems \[<a href="#bib.bib95" class="ltx_ref"
title="AFlow: automating agentic workflow generation">57</a>\]. Another
line of work searches over memory designs for continual-learning agents,
where memory persists across task
streams \[<a href="#bib.bib86" class="ltx_ref"
title="Memevolve: meta-evolution of agent memory systems">56</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib75" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to continually learn via meta-learning agentic memory designs">49</a>\].
In contrast, Meta-Harness searches over domain-specific harnesses,
including prompt construction, retrieval, and state update strategies
that reset between tasks. Its outer loop is deliberately minimal:
instead of relying on a fixed scaffold, an archive of prior discoveries,
or a persistent memory mechanism, it gives the proposer unrestricted
filesystem access to prior experience. This lets the agent decide what
information to inspect and enables search over full harness
implementations rather than a predefined space of context-management
procedures.

</div>

<div id="S2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Text optimization
methods.</span> Meta-Harness is also closely related to methods such as
ProTeGi, TextGrad, OPRO, GEPA, AlphaEvolve/OpenEvolve, and Feedback
Descent, which iteratively improve prompts or other text artifacts using
feedback from prior attempts \[<a href="#bib.bib55" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automatic prompt optimization with “gradient descent” and beam search">37</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib43" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-refine: iterative refinement with self-feedback">30</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib81" class="ltx_ref"
title="TextGrad: automatic ”differentiation” via text">52</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib76" class="ltx_ref"
title="Large language models as optimizers">50</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">34</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="OpenEvolve: an open-source evolutionary coding agent">42</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib40" class="ltx_ref"
title="Feedback descent: open-ended text optimization via pairwise comparison">25</a>\].
However, these methods are less well suited to harness engineering,
where optimization targets a complete executable procedure, and the
relevant environmental feedback is distributed across code, scores, and
execution traces in a way that is hard to summarize up front. Rather
than reacting only to aggregate scores or summaries, the proposer in
Meta-Harness can reason over failed examples and their execution traces
to propose targeted edits. See <a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a> for a comparison of problem
scale considered in those papers and ours,
and <a href="#S0.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figures</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>
and <a href="#A0.F4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 4 ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> for a direct comparison with
OpenEvolve, GEPA, and TTT-Discover in our problem setting.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Meta-Harness: A Harness for Optimizing Harnesses

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This section describes Meta-Harness, our outer-loop procedure for
searching over task-specific harnesses. Meta-Harness is built on the
idea that harness optimization benefits from allowing a proposer to
selectively inspect prior code and execution traces via filesystem
access, rather than optimizing from lossy summaries or an additional
hand-designed search structure. At a high level, it repeatedly proposes,
evaluates, and logs new harnesses.

</div>

<div id="S3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Meta-Harness is itself a harness in the broad sense (hence the name),
since it determines what information the proposer model sees during
search. Unless otherwise noted, we use *harness* to refer to the
task-specific programs being optimized.

</div>

<div id="S3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S3.p3.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Objective.</span> A
harness is a stateful program that wraps a language model and determines
what context the model sees at each step. The goal is simple: find the
harness that makes the underlying model perform best on the target task
distribution. Formally, let $`M`$ denote a fixed language model and
$`\mathcal{X}`$ a task distribution. For a harness $`H`$ and task
instance $`x\sim\mathcal{X}`$, we execute a rollout trajectory
$`\tau\sim p_{M}(H,x)`$. The harness constructs prompts for $`M`$, the
model responds, and the harness updates its state after each
interaction. A task-specific reward function $`r(\tau,x)`$ scores the
trajectory. The objective of harness optimization is to
<span id="S3.p3.7.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">find the harness
that maximizes the expected final reward</span>:

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
H^{*}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{H}\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mathcal{X},\tau\sim p_{M}(H,x)}\;r(\tau,x),
``` |  |

When multiple objectives are relevant (e.g., accuracy and context cost),
we evaluate candidates under Pareto dominance and report the resulting
frontier. In practice, this search has traditionally been carried out by
human engineers and researchers, who iteratively refine prompts,
context-management rules, and tool-use logic by hand.

</div>

<div id="S3.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness search
loop.</span> Meta-Harness uses a single coding-agent proposer with
access to a growing filesystem $`\mathcal{D}`$ that serves as its
feedback channel<span id="footnote1"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>Based
on earlier exploration, we think this workflow only became practical
recently, following major improvements in coding-agent capabilities
around early 2026.</span></span></span>. Here, a <span id="S3.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">coding agent</span> is a
language-model-based system that can invoke developer tools and modify
code. Unlike prior systems that externalize the improvement logic in a
hand-designed search loop, Meta-Harness delegates diagnosis and proposal
to the coding agent itself: it decides which prior artifacts to inspect,
which failure modes to address, and whether to make a local edit or a
more substantial rewrite. Equivalently, the proposer is not a raw
next-token model operating on a fixed prompt assembled by the outer
loop; it is an agent that retrieves information, navigates prior
artifacts, and edits code as part of the search itself. Each evaluated
harness contributes a directory containing its source code, scores, and
execution traces (such as prompts, tool calls, model outputs, and state
updates). The filesystem is typically far larger than the proposer’s
context window, so the proposer queries it through terminal tools such
as <span id="S3.p4.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">grep</span>
and <span id="S3.p4.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">cat</span>
rather than ingesting it as a single prompt. At each iteration, the
proposer first inspects prior code, scores, and execution traces, then
reasons about likely failure modes before generating a new harness.

</div>

<div id="S3.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Meta-Harness maintains a population $`\mathcal{H}`$ and a Pareto
frontier over evaluated harnesses, but imposes no parent-selection rule:
the proposer is free to inspect <span id="S3.p5.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">any</span> prior harness and its
execution trace when proposing new ones. We run evolution for a fixed
number of iterations and perform a final test-set evaluation on the
Pareto frontier. This simplicity is deliberate: by leaving diagnosis and
edit decisions to the proposer rather than hard-coding search
heuristics, Meta-Harness can improve automatically as coding agents
become more capable. The proposer never sees test-set results; its only
feedback comes from the <span id="S3.p5.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">search set</span>, the subset of task
instances used to evaluate candidate harnesses during search and
generate the feedback signal for improvement, and from execution traces
logged during those search runs.

</div>

<div id="S3.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S3.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Advantages of
code-space search.</span> Harness optimization occurs in code space,
where small changes to retrieval, memory, or prompt-construction logic
can affect behavior many steps later, making local search heuristics
poorly matched to the problem. By inspecting execution traces, the
proposer can often infer *why* a harness failed and which earlier design
choices likely contributed to the failure, not just *that* it failed, as
illustrated by the search trajectories in <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendices</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>
and <a href="#A1.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="A.2 Qualitative Behavior: Causal Reasoning Over Prior Failures ‣ Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A.2</span></a>. There, we see that the
proposer reads broadly across prior code and logs, then uses those
traces to identify confounded edits, isolate likely causal changes, and
shift toward safer modifications after repeated regressions. The
proposer can therefore modify the harness at the level of algorithmic
structure, ranging from changes to retrieval, memory, or
prompt-construction logic to full program rewrites, rather than filling
in templates or applying predefined mutation operators. In practice, it
often starts from a strong prior harness, but this is an emergent
strategy rather than a hard-coded rule. Although the search space is
large, representing harnesses as programs provides a natural
regularization bias: coding models tend to propose coherent algorithms
rather than brittle, hard-coded solutions, which biases the search
toward reusable context-management procedures. This action space is
closely aligned with the read–write–execute workflows on which frontier
coding assistants are trained.

</div>

<div id="S3.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S3.p7.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Practical
implementation.</span> In our experiments, each harness is a single-file
Python program that modifies task-specific prompting, retrieval, memory,
and orchestration logic. In our experiments, the proposer $`P`$ is
Claude Code \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Claude code: an agentic coding tool">4</a>\] with
<span id="S3.p7.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Opus-4.6</span>. The proposer is
guided by a minimal domain-specific skill that describes where to write
new harnesses, how to inspect previous harnesses and their execution
traces, and what files it can and cannot modify. The base model $`M`$
varies by domain and is always frozen; see <a href="#S4" class="ltx_ref"
title="4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> for details. In our
experiments, a typical run evaluates roughly 60 harnesses over 20
iterations. We provide additional tips for implementing Meta-Harness in
a new domain in <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Practical Implementation Tips ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>.

</div>

<figure id="alg1"
class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Experiments

<div id="S4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We evaluate Meta-Harness on three task domains: online text
classification, math reasoning, and agentic coding. In each domain, we
compare harnesses discovered by our search against domain-appropriate
baselines using the standard evaluation metric. Please refer to each
subsection for the precise experimental setup.

</div>

<div id="S4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We compare against two main classes of methods. <span id="S4.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(1) Human-designed strategies</span>:
these are hand-crafted harnesses for each domain, representing the
current state of the art in context construction. We describe these
baselines in the corresponding subsections. <span id="S4.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">(2) Program-search methods:</span> these
methods search over candidate harnesses using feedback and reward
signals, but are designed for smaller-scale settings than harness
engineering.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Online Text Classification

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We follow the online text classification setup of
Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
\[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agentic context engineering: evolving contexts for self-improving language models">58</a>\];
Ye<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
\[<a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref"
title="Meta context engineering via agentic skill evolution">51</a>\]:
an LLM receives labeled examples one at a time, updates its memory, and
is evaluated on a held-out test set. We use <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GPT-OSS-120B</span> as the LLM text
classifier, and consider the problem of designing a harness for text
classification. We use three datasets, chosen for difficulty and domain
diversity: <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LawBench</span>
(Law) \[<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Lawbench: benchmarking legal knowledge of large language models">15</a>\]
predicts criminal charges from case descriptions (215 classes);
<span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Symptom2Disease</span>
(S2D) \[<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref"
title="Symptom to diagnosis dataset">18</a>\] predicts diseases from
symptom descriptions (22 classes); and <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">USPTO-50k</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib58" class="ltx_ref"
title="What’s what: the (nearly) definitive guide to reaction role assignment">40</a>\]
predicts precursor reactants from product molecules (180 classes). We
initialize the search population $`\mathcal{H}`$ from the main baseline
harnesses in this setting: zero-shot, few-shot, ACE, and MCE. We ran 20
evolution iterations with two candidates per iteration, producing 40
candidate harnesses.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Table 2: </span>
Test-set metrics for all harnesses on the three datasets. Ctx denotes
additional input tokens in context (thousands). †: implementation from
Ye<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span> [<a
href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref"
title="Meta context engineering via agentic skill evolution">51</a>].
<span class="math inline">↓</span>: lower is better. <span
id="S4.T2.4.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness improves
online text classification accuracy while using a smaller input
context.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S4.F3.35"
class="ltx_figure ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_bottom"
style="width:199.5pt;">
<img src="/html/2603.28052/assets/x4.png" id="S4.F3.35.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="825"
height="528" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 3: </span>
Pareto frontier of accuracy vs. context tokens on online text
classification. <span id="S4.F3.35.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness achieves a stronger
accuracy-context Pareto frontier than all comparison
methods.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS1.p2.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comparison vs
text optimizers.</span> We compare Meta-Harness against representative
methods for optimizing text. For a fair comparison, we use the same
proposer configuration (<span id="S4.SS1.p2.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Opus-4.6</span> with max
reasoning), select candidates solely based on search-set performance,
and hold out the test sets until the final evaluation. Since evaluation
is the main computational bottleneck, we give each method the same
budget of proposal harness evaluations. We consider the following points
of comparison:

- <span id="S4.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i1.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Best-of-N</span>: independent samples
  from the seed with no search structure; a compute-matched control for
  whether search matters at all.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i2.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">OpenEvolve</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
  title="OpenEvolve: an open-source evolutionary coding agent">42</a>\]:
  evolutionary search over programs with LLM mutation.

  </div>
- <span id="S4.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S4.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="S4.I1.i3.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TTT-Discover</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib82" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Learning to discover at test time">53</a>\]: we use only the
  text-optimization component of their method, i.e., proposal selection
  via the PUCT reuse rule.

  </div>

<span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">In this setting,
Meta-Harness matches the best prior text optimizers (OpenEvolve,
TTT-Discover) in $`0.1\times`$ the evaluations</span>, and its final
accuracy surpasses theirs by more than 10 points
(<a href="#S0.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figures</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>
and <a href="#S4.T4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 4 ‣ 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>). We attribute this speedup to
the intentional design choices that impose minimum necessary structure
on the outer loop (<a href="#S3" class="ltx_ref"
title="3 Meta-Harness: A Harness for Optimizing Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). In particular, Meta-Harness
preserves <span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">full
experience history using a filesystem</span> and allows the proposer to
inspect anything necessary, whereas both OpenEvolve and TTT-Discover
operate with more structured and substantially more limited proposer
inputs than full filesystem access. We note that online text
classification is the smallest-context setting we study
(<a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>), so if structure-heavy text
optimizers already lag here, their limitations may only grow in harder
regimes.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 3: </span>
Ablation of the information available to the proposer in online text
classification. <span class="math inline">&gt;</span> ZS: number of runs
whose accuracy exceeded the zero-shot baseline. The full Meta-Harness
interface substantially outperforms scores-only and scores-plus-summary
ablations. <span id="S4.T3.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Access to
raw execution traces is the key ingredient for enabling harness
search.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To isolate which parts of the proposer interface matter most, we compare
three conditions in online text classification: a scores-only condition,
a scores-plus-summary condition in which the proposer receives
LLM-generated summaries but no raw traces, and the full
<span id="S4.SS1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness</span> interface with
access to execution traces (<a href="#S4.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>). The results show a large gap
in favor of the full interface: scores-only reaches 34.6 median and 41.3
best accuracy, while scores-plus-summary reaches 34.9 median and 38.7
best. By contrast, <span id="S4.SS1.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness</span> reaches 50.0
median and 56.7 best accuracy, and even its median candidate outperforms
the best candidate found under either ablation. We interpret this as
evidence that full access to execution traces is the most important
component of the interface: summaries do not recover the missing signal,
and may even hurt by compressing away diagnostically useful details.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 4: </span> Text
classification accuracies of the harnesses proposed by different text
optimizers (search set). <span id="S4.T4.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness is substantially more
effective at harness optimization.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS1.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Comparison vs
state-of-the-art harnesses.</span> Our primary points of comparison are
hand-designed harnesses for this problem setting: Agentic Context
Engineering (ACE, Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib85" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agentic context engineering: evolving contexts for self-improving language models">58</a>\]),
which uses reflective memory curation to build context over time, and
Meta Context Engineering (MCE, Ye<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib79" class="ltx_ref"
title="Meta context engineering via agentic skill evolution">51</a>\]),
which maintains and evolves a library of natural-language skills for
context construction. As additional baselines, we evaluate zero-shot
prompting and few-shot prompting with $`N\in\{4,8,16,32,\text{all}\}`$
examples. Results in <a href="#S4.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Figure 3 ‣ 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> show that Meta-Harness
improves substantially over prior hand-designed harnesses. The selected
<span id="S4.SS1.p5.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness<span id="footnote2"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>2</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note"><span id="footnote2.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_serif">2</span></span><span id="footnote2.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_serif">We slightly overload terminology for
brevity: in the tables, </span><span id="footnote2.10"
class="ltx_text">Meta-Harness</span><span id="footnote2.11"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_serif"> denotes the best discovered harness,
whereas elsewhere it refers to the entire harness search
procedure.</span></span></span></span></span> reaches 48.6% accuracy,
outperforming ACE by 7.7 points and MCE by 8.6 points. These gains do
not come from using more context: <span id="S4.SS1.p5.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness</span> uses only 11.4K
context tokens, versus 50.8K for ACE and 28.5K for MCE.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS1.p6.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Accuracy–Context
Tradeoffs.</span> Because Meta-Harness performs free-form optimization
over harness code, we can express a joint preference for both accuracy
and context cost rather than committing to a single scalar objective in
advance. Given only the current metrics and the desired trade-off, the
proposer is able to discover harnesses across a broad range of the
frontier, yielding a smooth accuracy–context Pareto curve
in <a href="#S4.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a>. This allows us to trade
additional context for higher test accuracy in a controlled way, rather
than committing to a single hand-designed operating point.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS1.p7.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Out-of-distribution (OOD) task
evaluation.</span> We evaluate whether the discovered harness
generalizes to entirely new datasets unseen during search. We consider
nine diverse datasets, and describe them in detail
in <a href="#A3.SS1" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.1 OOD Text Classification Datasets ‣ Appendix C Dataset Details ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.1</span></a>. The selected
<span id="S4.SS1.p7.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness</span> system achieves
the best average accuracy (73.1%), outperforming ACE (70.2%) and all
few-shot baselines (<a href="#S4.T5" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>). Notably, we observe that
naively adding more few-shot examples beyond $`32`$ hurts performance in
$`7/9`$ tasks. Meta-Harness shows the highest performance on 6/9
datasets, suggesting that the discovered harness captures generally
effective strategies for text classification rather than overfitting to
the specific datasets used during search.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.2 </span>Harnesses for Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning

<div id="S4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

We study a somewhat non-standard setup for olympiad math solving:
augmenting the model with the ability to retrieve examples from a large
corpus. There is a good reason to expect retrieval to help mathematical
reasoning in principle, because solutions often share reusable proof
patterns, so previous reasoning traces contain information that a model
may be able to exploit at inference time. Yet retrieval has not become a
standard ingredient in this setting, and prior work suggests that it has
been much less successful on reasoning-intensive math benchmarks than in
more fact-grounded domains \[<a href="#bib.bib99" class="ltx_ref"
title="Adaptive retrieval helps reasoning in llms – but mostly if it’s not used">41</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib100" class="ltx_ref"
title="RAR-b: reasoning as retrieval benchmark">48</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib98" class="ltx_ref"
title="MathArena: evaluating llms on uncontaminated math competitions">5</a>\].
The difficulty is that naive retrieval rarely surfaces the right traces
in the right form. This suggests that success depends less on adding
retrieval per se than on discovering the right retrieval policy. Rather
than hand-designing that policy, we give Meta-Harness a hard set of
olympiad problems and allow the retrieval behavior itself to emerge from
search.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The retrieval corpus contains $`\geq`$<!-- -->500,000 solved problems
from eight open-source datasets. We carefully deduplicated and
decontaminated it against both evaluation benchmarks and the search set,
confirmed that held-out problems have no exact prefix matches under our
string-based filter, and manually inspected top BM25 retrievals for
held-out examples (<a href="#A3.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="C.2 Math Retrieval Corpus ‣ Appendix C Dataset Details ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">C.2</span></a>). We use Meta-Harness to
optimize a harness for 40 iterations over a 250-problem search set of
Olympiad-difficulty math problems (OlympiadBench + Omni-MATH hard),
producing 109 candidate retrieval harnesses. We initialize the search
population $`\mathcal{H}`$ from the main baseline harnesses in this
setting: zero-shot, few-shot, and ACE. We select a single harness based
on search-set performance using <span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GPT-OSS-20B</span>
(<a href="#A2.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="B.2 Math Retrieval Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">B.2</span></a>). We evaluate this harness
on $`200`$ previously unseen IMO-level problems drawn from
IMO-AnswerBench, IMO-ProofBench, and
ArXivMath \[<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="Towards robust mathematical reasoning">29</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib98" class="ltx_ref"
title="MathArena: evaluating llms on uncontaminated math competitions">5</a>\].
In addition to <span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GPT-OSS-20B</span>, we evaluate the
same retrieval harness on four models not seen during search:
<span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GPT-5.4-nano</span>,
<span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">GPT-5.4-mini</span>,
<span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemini-3.1-Flash-Lite</span>, and
<span id="S4.SS2.p2.3.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Gemini-3-Flash</span>. We follow
the standard evaluation protocol of prior
work \[<a href="#bib.bib35" class="ltx_ref"
title="Towards robust mathematical reasoning">29</a>\] and report
accuracy averaged over three samples per problem.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results.</span>
<a href="#S4.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.2 Harnesses for Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> compares the discovered
harness against no retrieval, dense retrieval using the separate
embedding model <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">text-embedding-3-small</span>,
random few-shot prompting, and BM25 retrieval. In contrast, Meta-Harness
operates entirely in code space on top of the same BM25-based lexical
retrieval stack as the sparse baseline, rather than introducing an
additional dense encoder. The discovered retrieval harness outperforms
the no-retrieval baseline across all five held-out models, with an
average gain of <span id="S4.SS2.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">4.7 points</span>. It also matches or
exceeds the strongest fixed baselines on average, outperforming BM25
retrieval by 1.3 points overall, while avoiding the regressions observed
with dense retrieval and random few-shot prompting across several
models.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table">Table 6: </span>
Retrieval-augmented math problem solving on 200 IMO-level math problems.
We show pass@1 averaged over three samples per problem, with absolute
improvement over the baseline in parentheses. <span id="S4.T6.4.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The discovered Meta-Harness retrieval
strategy improves reasoning on these IMO-level problems across all five
held-out models, with a 4.7-point average gain over no
retriever.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.3 </span>Evaluating Agentic Coding Harnesses on TerminalBench-2

<div id="S4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

TerminalBench-2 \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminal-bench: benchmarking agents on hard, realistic tasks in command line interfaces">32</a>\]
evaluates LLM agents on 89 challenging tasks that require long-horizon,
fully autonomous execution under complex dependencies, and substantial
domain knowledge. Prior work has shown that the choice agent harness has
a large effect on performance on this benchmark. We initialize search
from two strong open baselines,
Terminus 2 \[<a href="#bib.bib46" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminal-bench: benchmarking agents on hard, realistic tasks in command line interfaces">32</a>\]
and Terminus-KIRA \[<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminus-kira: boosting frontier model performance on terminal-bench with minimal harness">24</a>\].
For this experiment, we perform search and final evaluation on the same
89-task benchmark. We use this benchmark as a <span id="S4.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">discovery
problem</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib83" class="ltx_ref"
title="Learning to discover at test time">54</a>\] in which the goal is
to discover a harness configuration that improves performance on a hard,
publicly contested benchmark. This is standard practice: public writeups
already describe repeated benchmark-specific harness iteration on
TerminalBench itself \[<a href="#bib.bib69" class="ltx_ref"
title="Benchmarks don’t matter">17</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib68" class="ltx_ref"
title="How we scored #1 on terminal-bench (52%)">33</a>;
<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminus-kira: boosting frontier model performance on terminal-bench with minimal harness">24</a>\],
and the benchmark is small and expensive enough that introducing a
separate split would materially weaken the search signal. We
additionally check for overfitting by manual inspection and regex-based
audits for task-specific string leakage into evolved harnesses. We note
that although the resulting harness is specialized to the
TerminalBench-2 regime, autonomous completion of difficult long-horizon
tasks from a single instruction is a core capability, and the benchmark
consists of many tasks that frontier models and heavily engineered
harnesses struggle with.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results.</span>
We report results on the full benchmark
in <a href="#S4.T7" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.3 Evaluating Agentic Coding Harnesses on TerminalBench-2 ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">7</span></a>, evaluated on two base models:
<span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Claude
Opus 4.6</span> and <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Claude Haiku 4.5</span>. On
<span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Opus
4.6</span>, Meta-Harness discovers a harness achieving 76.4% pass rate,
surpassing the hand-engineered Terminus-KIRA (74.7%) and ranking \#2
among all <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Opus 4.6</span> agents on the
TerminalBench-2 leaderboard. The only higher-scoring
<span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.6" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Opus
4.6</span> agent is ForgeCode (81.8%); however, we were unable to
reproduce their reported result from the publicly available code alone,
suggesting their leaderboard scores depend on components beyond the
published repository. On the weaker <span id="S4.SS3.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Haiku 4.5</span> model, the
improvement is larger: Meta-Harness achieves 37.6%, outperforming the
next-best reported agent (Goose, 35.5%) by 2.1 points. TerminalBench-2
is an actively contested benchmark with multiple teams directly
optimizing for it, so the fact that an automatic search method can
achieve benefits at this frontier is encouraging for long-horizon
text-optimization loops.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="S4.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Qualitative
behavior of the proposer.</span> The harness search trajectory helps
explain <span id="S4.SS3.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">why</span> Meta-Harness achieves these
gains; we provide a detailed summary in <a href="#A1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A</span></a>. In early iterations, the
proposer combined plausible structural fixes with prompt-template edits
and observed that both candidates regressed. It then explicitly
hypothesized that the regressions were confounded by the shared prompt
intervention, isolated the structural changes from the prompt rewrite,
and ultimately pivoted toward a safer additive modification that became
the best candidate in the run. This provides qualitative evidence that
<span id="S4.SS3.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">filesystem
access enables the proposer to inspect prior experience in enough detail
to form causal hypotheses and revise the harness accordingly.</span>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Discussion

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Beyond outperforming existing harnesses, Meta-Harness has several
practical advantages. Discovered harnesses generalize to
out-of-distribution classification datasets
(<a href="#S4.T5" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.1 Online Text Classification ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>) and to unseen base models in
the math setting (<a href="#S4.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 4.2 Harnesses for Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a>). A search run completes in a
few hours of wall-clock time, yet produces readable, transferable
strategies that can be reused across models, including future, stronger
ones. Overfitting in code space is also more inspectable: brittle
if-chains or hard-coded class mappings are visible on inspection in a
way that weight-space overfitting is not. More broadly, our results
suggest that the main advantage of Meta-Harness is not just search over
code, but search with *selective access to prior diagnostic experience*.
The proposer is not limited to scalar rewards or fixed summaries; it can
inspect raw code, execution traces, and prior failures, then use that
information to form and test hypotheses about what to change. The
qualitative search trajectories in <a href="#A1.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="A.2 Qualitative Behavior: Causal Reasoning Over Prior Failures ‣ Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">A.2</span></a> illustrate this behavior
directly.

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Our findings reflect a recurring pattern in machine
learning \[<a href="#bib.bib64" class="ltx_ref"
title="The bitter lesson, 2019">44</a>\]: once a search space becomes
accessible, stronger general-purpose agents can outperform
hand-engineered solutions. A natural next step for future work is to
co-evolve the harness and the model weights, letting the strategy shape
what the model learns and vice versa. While we evaluate on three diverse
domains, our experiments demonstrate that harness search can work with
one particularly strong coding-agent proposer (Claude Code); a broader
study of how the effect varies across proposer agents remains for future
work.

</div>

<figure id="A0.F4" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="/html/2603.28052/assets/x5.png" id="A0.F4.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="821"
height="505" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 4: </span>
Search-set accuracy over evaluations for all compared text optimizers on
online text classification. Each point is one candidate harness; lines
track the best-so-far. Per-dataset curves are shown alongside the
aggregate. <span id="A0.F4.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Meta-Harness reaches the final accuracy
of OpenEvolve and TTT-Discover within the first 4 evaluations and
continues improving, ending more than 10 points above all
baselines.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Qualitative Proposer Behavior

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This section examines how the proposer uses the filesystem during
search, drawing on the TerminalBench-2 run (10 iterations,
<span id="A1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Claude Opus
4.6</span>).

</div>

<div id="A1.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A.1 </span>File Access Statistics

<div id="A1.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

To verify that the proposer makes substantive use of the filesystem
rather than defaulting to local edits, we recorded all file reads per
iteration.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<a href="#A1.T8" class="ltx_ref"
title="In A.1 File Access Statistics ‣ Appendix A Qualitative Proposer Behavior ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> summarizes the results. The
proposer reads a median of 82 files per iteration (range 69–99), roughly
evenly split between prior harness source code (41%) and execution
traces (40%), with the remainder going to score summaries (6%) and other
files (13%). This confirms that the proposer’s access pattern is
non-Markovian: it routinely inspects the majority of available history
rather than conditioning only on the most recent parent.

</div>

</tr>
</thead>

class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_row ltx_border_t">Files read
per iteration (median)</td>
<td id="A1.T8.1.2.1.2"
class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_right ltx_border_t">82</td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T8.1.3.2" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="A1.T8.1.3.2.1"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_left ltx_th ltx_th_row">Files read per iteration
(range)</td>
<td id="A1.T8.1.3.2.2"
class="ltx_td ltx_nopad_r ltx_align_right">69–99</td>
</tr>
<tr id="A1.T8.1.4.3" class="ltx_tr">
<td colspan="2" id="A1.T8.1.4.3.1"

<div id="A1.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">A.2 </span>Qualitative Behavior: Causal Reasoning Over Prior Failures

<div id="A1.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The TerminalBench-2 search log reveals a clear narrative arc in which
the proposer learns from its own regressions. Rather than wandering
randomly through local edits, it forms an explicit diagnosis of why
early candidates failed, then shifts toward a safer design pattern. All
text inside the log boxes below is quoted verbatim from the proposer’s
recorded reasoning at each iteration (emphasis ours).

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iterations 1–2:
promising bugfixes are confounded by prompt edits.</span> The first two
iterations both bundle plausible structural fixes with prompt-template
modifications, and both regress sharply from the 64.4% Terminus-KIRA
baseline. Iteration 1 targets observation corruption from leaked
terminal markers and adds a loop breaker:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnAzLnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSI4NS40NSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIHZlcnNpb249IjEuMSIgdmlld2JveD0iMCAwIDYwMCA4NS40NSIgd2lkdGg9IjYwMCI+PGcgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LXN0cm9rZS1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZpbGwtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDsiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0idHJhbnNsYXRlKDAsODUuNDUpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40cHQiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNCM0IzQjM7IiBmaWxsPSIjQjNCM0IzIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgNS45MSBMIDAgNzkuNTUgQyAwIDgyLjgxIDIuNjQgODUuNDUgNS45MSA4NS40NSBMIDU5NC4wOSA4NS40NSBDIDU5Ny4zNiA4NS40NSA2MDAgODIuODEgNjAwIDc5LjU1IEwgNjAwIDUuOTEgQyA2MDAgMi42NCA1OTcuMzYgMCA1OTQuMDkgMCBMIDUuOTEgMCBDIDIuNjQgMCAwIDIuNjQgMCA1LjkxIFoiIC8+PC9nPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNGQUZBRkE7IiBmaWxsPSIjRkFGQUZBIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDEuOTcgNS45MSBMIDEuOTcgNzkuNTUgQyAxLjk3IDgxLjcyIDMuNzMgODMuNDkgNS45MSA4My40OSBMIDU5NC4wOSA4My40OSBDIDU5Ni4yNyA4My40OSA1OTguMDMgODEuNzIgNTk4LjAzIDc5LjU1IEwgNTk4LjAzIDUuOTEgQyA1OTguMDMgMy43MyA1OTYuMjcgMS45NyA1OTQuMDkgMS45NyBMIDUuOTEgMS45NyBDIDMuNzMgMS45NyAxLjk3IDMuNzMgMS45NyA1LjkxIFoiIC8+PC9nPjxnIGZpbGwtb3BhY2l0eT0iMS4wIiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxLjAgMC4wIDAuMCAxLjAgMTQuMjEgMTQuNTIpIj48Zm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdCBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmctY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1mby13aWR0aDo0MS4zMWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWhlaWdodDo0LjNlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjIyZW07IiB3aWR0aD0iNTcxLjU4IiBoZWlnaHQ9IjYyLjU3IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDU5LjUpIiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgY29sb3I9IiMwMDAwMDAiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IkExLlNTMi5wMy5waWMxLjEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozOS4zNGVtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJBMS5TUzIucDMucGljMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9wIj48c3BhbiBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnAzLnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY </div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

That candidate also introduced a new cleanup-oriented prompt template
and a verification checklist. Iteration 2 proposes a different
state-machine fix:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p5" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnA1LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDAuODMiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA2MDAgMTAwLjgzIiB3aWR0aD0iNjAwIj48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJ0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCwxMDAuODMpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40cHQiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNCM0IzQjM7IiBmaWxsPSIjQjNCM0IzIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgNS45MSBMIDAgOTQuOTIgQyAwIDk4LjE5IDIuNjQgMTAwLjgzIDUuOTEgMTAwLjgzIEwgNTk0LjA5IDEwMC44MyBDIDU5Ny4zNiAxMDAuODMgNjAwIDk4LjE5IDYwMCA5NC45MiBMIDYwMCA1LjkxIEMgNjAwIDIuNjQgNTk3LjM2IDAgNTk0LjA5IDAgTCA1LjkxIDAgQyAyLjY0IDAgMCAyLjY0IDAgNS45MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojRkFGQUZBOyIgZmlsbD0iI0ZBRkFGQSIgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSAxLjk3IDUuOTEgTCAxLjk3IDk0LjkyIEMgMS45NyA5Ny4xIDMuNzMgOTguODYgNS45MSA5OC44NiBMIDU5NC4wOSA5OC44NiBDIDU5Ni4yNyA5OC44NiA1OTguMDMgOTcuMSA1OTguMDMgOTQuOTIgTCA1OTguMDMgNS45MSBDIDU5OC4wMyAzLjczIDU5Ni4yNyAxLjk3IDU5NC4wOSAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAxNC4yMSAxNC41MikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mZy1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjQxLjMxZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjUuNDFlbTstLWx0eC1mby1kZXB0aDowLjIyZW07IiB3aWR0aD0iNTcxLjU4IiBoZWlnaHQ9Ijc3Ljk1IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09Im1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDc0Ljg3KSIgb3ZlcmZsb3c9InZpc2libGUiIGNvbG9yPSIjMDAwMDAwIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGFpbmVyIj48c3BhbiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2ZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3RfY29udGVudCI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJBMS5TUzIucDUucGljMS4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozOS4zNGVtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJBMS5TUzIucDUucGljMS4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuMi4yLjIuM id="A1.SS2.p5.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p6" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This second candidate removes the pending-completion mechanism entirely,
while also carrying over the marker stripping and the new prompt. It
still regresses, which gives the proposer two failed candidates with
different structural changes but one shared prompt intervention.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p7" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p7.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iteration 3: the
proposer identifies the confound.</span> By iteration 3, the proposer
explicitly infers that the regressions are not primarily due to the
structural bugfixes themselves:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p8" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnA4LnBpYzEiIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfcGljdHVyZSIgaGVpZ2h0PSIxMDIuMDYiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA2MDAgMTAyLjA2IiB3aWR0aD0iNjAwIj48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtc3Ryb2tlLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOyIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJ0cmFuc2xhdGUoMCwxMDIuMDYpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIiBmaWxsPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2U9IiMwMDAwMDAiIHN0cm9rZS13aWR0aD0iMC40cHQiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiNCM0IzQjM7IiBmaWxsPSIjQjNCM0IzIiBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCI+PHBhdGggc3R5bGU9InN0cm9rZTpub25lIiBkPSJNIDAgNS45MSBMIDAgOTYuMTUgQyAwIDk5LjQyIDIuNjQgMTAyLjA2IDUuOTEgMTAyLjA2IEwgNTk0LjA5IDEwMi4wNiBDIDU5Ny4zNiAxMDIuMDYgNjAwIDk5LjQyIDYwMCA5Ni4xNSBMIDYwMCA1LjkxIEMgNjAwIDIuNjQgNTk3LjM2IDAgNTk0LjA5IDAgTCA1LjkxIDAgQyAyLjY0IDAgMCAyLjY0IDAgNS45MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojRkFGQUZBOyIgZmlsbD0iI0ZBRkFGQSIgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSAxLjk3IDUuOTEgTCAxLjk3IDk2LjE1IEMgMS45NyA5OC4zMyAzLjczIDEwMC4wOSA1LjkxIDEwMC4wOSBMIDU5NC4wOSAxMDAuMDkgQyA1OTYuMjcgMTAwLjA5IDU5OC4wMyA5OC4zMyA1OTguMDMgOTYuMTUgTCA1OTguMDMgNS45MSBDIDU5OC4wMyAzLjczIDU5Ni4yNyAxLjk3IDU5NC4wOSAxLjk3IEwgNS45MSAxLjk3IEMgMy43MyAxLjk3IDEuOTcgMy43MyAxLjk3IDUuOTEgWiIgLz48L2c+PGcgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEuMCAwLjAgMC4wIDEuMCAxNC4yMSAxNC41MikiPjxmb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0IHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1mZy1jb2xvcjojMDAwMDAwOy0tbHR4LWZvLXdpZHRoOjQxLjMxZW07LS1sdHgtZm8taGVpZ2h0OjUuNWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMjJlbTsiIHdpZHRoPSI1NzEuNTgiIGhlaWdodD0iNzkuMTgiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgNzYuMSkiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiBjb2xvcj0iIzAwMDAwMCI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRhaW5lciI+PHNwYW4gY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9mb3JlaWdub2JqZWN0X2NvbnRlbnQiPgo8c3BhbiBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnA4LnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X2lubGluZS1ibG9jayBsdHhfbWluaXBhZ2UgbHR4X2FsaWduX2JvdHRvbSIgc3R5bGU9IndpZHRoOjM5LjM0ZW07Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IkExLlNTMi5wOC5waWMxLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3AiPjxzcGFuIGlkPSJBMS5TUzIucDgucGljMS4xLjEuM id="A1.SS2.p8.pic1" class="ltx_picture" /></span>

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p9" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This is the key causal step in the trajectory. The proposer notices that
the common factor across the first two failures is not the particular
bugfix, but the cleanup-heavy prompt rewrite. It therefore reverts to
the original prompt and tests only the marker-stripping and
loop-breaker. The resulting candidate still underperforms slightly
(63.3%, -1.1pp), but it loses far less than the earlier versions, which
supports the confound diagnosis.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p10" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p10.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iterations 4–6:
direct fixes to the diagnosed failure mode still regress.</span> The
next three iterations continue to probe the same part of the design
space, but now with more explicit theories about why the completion
logic is fragile. Iteration 4 attributes failures to a concrete
state-machine bug in which verification commands reset the completion
flag and trap the agent in repeated checklist cycles:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p11" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img

<div id="A1.SS2.p12" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The proposer even cites concrete trajectory evidence, noting that
<span id="A1.SS2.p12.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">configure-git-webserver</span>
produced baseline failures with agents stuck in 30–60 step verification
spirals after effectively solving the task. Iteration 5 tries to soften
the cleanup language while preserving confirmation, but still edits the
prompt and regresses badly. Iteration 6 returns to the safer
<span id="A1.SS2.p12.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">evo_strip_only</span> base and
proposes a systems-level optimization:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p13" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img
src="data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iQTEuU1MyLnAxMy5waWMxIiBjbGFzcz0ibHR4X3BpY3R1cmUiIGhlaWdodD0iNjcuNjIiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCA2MDAgNjcuNjIiIHdpZHRoPSI2MDAiPjxnIHN0eWxlPSItLWx0eC1zdHJva2UtY29sb3I6IzAwMDAwMDstLWx0eC1maWxsLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7IiB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDY3LjYyKSBtYXRyaXgoMSAwIDAgLTEgMCAwKSIgZmlsbD0iIzAwMDAwMCIgc3Ryb2tlPSIjMDAwMDAwIiBzdHJva2Utd2lkdGg9IjAuNHB0Ij48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojQjNCM0IzOyIgZmlsbD0iI0IzQjNCMyIgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSAwIDUuOTEgTCAwIDYxLjcxIEMgMCA2NC45OCAyLjY0IDY3LjYyIDUuOTEgNjcuNjIgTCA1OTQuMDkgNjcuNjIgQyA1OTcuMzYgNjcuNjIgNjAwIDY0Ljk4IDYwMCA2MS43MSBMIDYwMCA1LjkxIEMgNjAwIDIuNjQgNTk3LjM2IDAgNTk0LjA5IDAgTCA1LjkxIDAgQyAyLjY0IDAgMCAyLjY0IDAgNS45MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBzdHlsZT0iLS1sdHgtZmlsbC1jb2xvcjojRkFGQUZBOyIgZmlsbD0iI0ZBRkFGQSIgZmlsbC1vcGFjaXR5PSIxLjAiPjxwYXRoIHN0eWxlPSJzdHJva2U6bm9uZSIgZD0iTSAxLjk3IDUuOTEgTCAxLjk3IDYxLjcxIEMgMS45NyA2My44OSAzLjczIDY1LjY1IDUuOTEgNjUuNjUgTCA1OTQuMDkgNjUuNjUgQyA1OTYuMjcgNjUuNjUgNTk4LjAzIDYzLjg5IDU5OC4wMyA2MS43MSBMIDU5OC4wMyA1LjkxIEMgNTk4LjAzIDMuNzMgNTk2LjI3IDEuOTcgNTk0LjA5IDEuOTcgTCA1LjkxIDEuOTcgQyAzLjczIDEuOTcgMS45NyAzLjczIDEuOTcgNS45MSBaIiAvPjwvZz48ZyBmaWxsLW9wYWNpdHk9IjEuMCIgdHJhbnNmb3JtPSJtYXRyaXgoMS4wIDAuMCAwLjAgMS4wIDE0LjIxIDE0LjUyKSI+PGZvcmVpZ25vYmplY3Qgc3R5bGU9Ii0tbHR4LWZnLWNvbG9yOiMwMDAwMDA7LS1sdHgtZm8td2lkdGg6NDEuMzFlbTstLWx0eC1mby1oZWlnaHQ6My4wMWVtOy0tbHR4LWZvLWRlcHRoOjAuMjJlbTsiIHdpZHRoPSI1NzEuNTgiIGhlaWdodD0iNDQuNzQiIHRyYW5zZm9ybT0ibWF0cml4KDEgMCAwIC0xIDAgNDEuNjYpIiBvdmVyZmxvdz0idmlzaWJsZSIgY29sb3I9IiMwMDAwMDAiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250YWluZXIiPjxzcGFuIGNsYXNzPSJsdHhfZm9yZWlnbm9iamVjdF9jb250ZW50Ij4KPHNwYW4gaWQ9IkExLlNTMi5wMTMucGljMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9pbmxpbmUtYmxvY2sgbHR4X21pbmlwYWdlIGx0eF9hbGlnbl9ib3R0b20iIHN0eWxlPSJ3aWR0aDozOS4zNGVtOyI+CjxzcGFuIGlkPSJBMS5TUzIucDEzLnBpYzEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuMS4xLjEuM <div id="A1.SS2.p14" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

That change also regresses. By this point, the proposer has learned a
specific empirical lesson: modifications to prompts and completion flow
are high risk, even when the local hypothesis sounds reasonable.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p15" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p15.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iteration 7:
the winning candidate.</span> After six consecutive regressions, the
proposer shifts strategy from modifying the control loop to adding
information before the loop begins:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p16" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<div id="A1.SS2.p17" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This candidate is the best result so far. The important point is not
just that iteration 7 wins, but that the proposer articulates *why* it
should be safer: it avoids touching the previously fragile completion
machinery and instead adds information that is useful mainly on hard
tasks.

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p18" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p18.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Iteration 8:
composition.</span> Having found one additive improvement, the proposer
next attempts to compose it with an earlier structural fix:

</div>

<div id="A1.SS2.p19" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span class="ltx_inline-block"><img

<div id="A1.SS2.p22" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<span id="A1.SS2.p22.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Summary.</span>
The search trajectory demonstrates that the proposer does more than
random mutation. Across the first seven iterations, it identifies a
confound, tests the confound-isolating hypothesis directly, observes
that control-flow and prompt edits remain fragile, and then deliberately
pivots to a purely additive modification that becomes the best candidate
in the run. It subsequently tries to compose that winning idea with
earlier fixes and even transfers lessons across runs. This kind of
causal reasoning over prior failures is precisely what full-history
filesystem access enables and what compressed-feedback optimizers cannot
support.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Discovered Harnesses

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Meta-Harness discovers executable inference-time procedures specific to
the problem setup at hand. These harnesses are structured,
domain-specific policies, often with nontrivial control flow such as
routing, filtering, and conditional context construction, selected
solely by whether they improve search-set performance. This section
presents compact, method-style abstractions of representative harnesses
that summarize the main behaviors and control-flow decisions that drive
inference-time behavior. For reference, the full implementation for each
discovered harness is on the order of 100–1000 lines of code.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.1 </span>Text Classification Harness

<div id="A2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

In online text classification, Meta-Harness discovers a family of
memory-based harnesses rather than a single canonical policy.
Table <a href="#A2.T9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 9 ‣ Meta-Harness (Label-Primed Query). ‣ B.1 Text Classification Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a> reports the Pareto frontier of
non-dominated variants from the main search, all selected solely by
search-set performance. We highlight two representative endpoints here:
<span id="A2.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness (Draft
Verification)</span>, the lowest-context frontier point, and
<span id="A2.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness (Label-Primed
Query)</span>, the highest-accuracy frontier point used in the main
text.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Overview.

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Both harnesses maintain a growing memory of past labeled examples and
build prompts from that memory at inference time. What differs is the
control flow used to interrogate the memory.
<span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness (Draft
Verification)</span> uses two short calls and explicitly tests the
model’s first guess against retrieved counterexamples, while
<span id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness (Label-Primed
Query)</span> spends a larger single-call budget on making the label
space and local decision boundaries explicit.
<a href="#A2.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Meta-Harness (Draft Verification). ‣ B.1 Text Classification Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figures</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a>
and <a href="#A2.F6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 6 ‣ Meta-Harness (Label-Primed Query). ‣ B.1 Text Classification Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> summarize these two programs.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Meta-Harness (Draft Verification).

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The corresponding discovered file is
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink ltx_ref_self">draft_verification.py</span>.
This lightweight variant turns prediction into a two-call procedure. It
first retrieves the 5 most similar labeled examples and makes a draft
prediction. It then re-queries the same memory conditioned on that draft
label, retrieving 5 *confirmers* with the same label and 5 *challengers*
with different labels, and asks the model whether to maintain or revise
its initial answer. The key discovered behavior is that the second
retrieval depends on both the query and the draft prediction, so the
harness can surface counterexamples targeted at the model’s current
guess rather than only generic near neighbors. If too few labeled
examples have been accumulated, the program falls back to a standard
single-call few-shot prompt.

</div>

<figure id="A2.F5" class="ltx_figure">
