id="A2.F5.pic1" class="ltx_picture ltx_centering" /></span>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 5: </span><span
id="A2.F5.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Draft-verification
classification harness.</span> The first call produces a draft label
from a short retrieved context. The second call retrieves evidence for
and against that draft and returns the final prediction.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px2.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

- <span id="A2.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Stage 1:
  Draft.</span> Retrieve the 5 nearest labeled examples and ask for an
  initial prediction.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Stage 2:
  Verification.</span> Condition retrieval on the draft label, then show
  both supporting and challenging examples before making the final
  prediction.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Cold
  start.</span> If fewer than 5 labeled examples are available, skip the
  two-stage procedure and use a standard single-call few-shot prompt.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Why it is
  cheap.</span> Both calls use short retrieved contexts, so the overall
  context cost stays near the low end of the frontier even with two
  model invocations.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Meta-Harness (Label-Primed Query).

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The corresponding discovered file is
<span class="ltx_ref ltx_nolink ltx_ref_self">label_primed_query_anchored.py</span>.
This strongest variant uses a single larger call built from three parts.
It begins with a *label primer* listing the valid output labels, then
constructs a *coverage* section with one query-relevant example per
label, and finally adds *query-anchored contrastive pairs* that place
highly similar examples with different labels side by side. The coverage
block exposes the full label space, while the contrastive block sharpens
local decision boundaries around the current query. In code, the harness
implements this with TF-IDF retrieval over past labeled examples and a
query-anchored pairing rule that chooses contrasting examples from the
same local neighborhood.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 6: </span><span
id="A2.F6.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Label-primed
query-anchored classification harness.</span> The program builds a
single prompt that exposes the label space, then populates it with
query-relevant coverage examples and local contrastive
pairs.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A2.SS1.SSS0.Px3.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="A2.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Label
  primer.</span> List the valid output labels before showing any
  examples, so the model sees the full answer space up front.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Coverage
  block.</span> For each known label, retrieve the most query-relevant
  labeled example and include one representative example per class.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I2.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Contrastive
  block.</span> Build pairs of highly similar examples with different
  labels, so the prompt exposes local decision boundaries around the
  current query.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I2.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I2.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I2.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Retrieval
  rule.</span> Use TF-IDF similarity and query-anchored partner
  selection rather than label-agnostic nearest neighbors.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.2 </span>Math Retrieval Harness

<div id="A2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This subsection describes the retrieval harness discovered by
Meta-Harness for mathematical reasoning
(<a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Harnesses for Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></a>). The final harness is a
compact four-route BM25 program whose structure emerged through search
rather than being manually specified after the fact. All design choices
below—the routing predicates, reranking terms, deduplication thresholds,
and per-route example counts—were selected by the outer loop across 40
iterations of evolution.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Overview.

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

At inference time, the harness assigns each problem to exactly one of
four routes: combinatorics, geometry, number theory, or a default route
for algebra and other problems. The gates are implemented as lightweight
lexical predicates over the problem statement, including keyword sets
and a small number of regex features for geometry notation. The harness
does not aggregate outputs across routes: once a route is selected, only
that route retrieves examples for the final prompt. All routes use BM25
as the underlying retrieval mechanism over the filtered corpus described
above. The BM25 index uses a math-aware tokenizer that preserves LaTeX
tokens (e.g., <span id="A2.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">\frac</span>,
<span id="A2.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">ˆ{2}</span>) as atomic units. The
selected harness is a merge of two successful search lineages,
autonomously combined by the proposer during search: one contributed a
stronger geometry route based on raw BM25, while another contributed a
stronger combinatorics route based on deduplication and difficulty
reranking. <a href="#A2.F8" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Overview. ‣ B.2 Math Retrieval Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">8</span></a> gives a compact flowchart view
of the final program.

</div>

<figure id="A2.F8" class="ltx_figure">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 8: </span><span
id="A2.F8.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Discovered math retrieval
harness.</span> A lexical router assigns each query to one of four
subject-specific retrieval policies. The selected policy retrieves
examples, which are inserted into the final prompt.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A2.SS2.SSS0.Px1.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

- <span id="A2.I3.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I3.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I3.i1.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Combinatorics:</span> fetch 20 BM25
  candidates, deduplicate to 8, rerank by lexical score and difficulty,
  then return the top 3. This is the main route where the harness
  explicitly trades off diversity against hard-problem matching.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I3.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I3.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I3.i2.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Geometry:</span> return 1 hard
  NuminaMath reference together with 2 raw BM25 neighbors. Search
  consistently prefers raw structural matches here over difficulty
  reranking.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I3.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I3.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I3.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Number
  theory:</span> fetch 12 BM25 candidates and rerank using lexical
  score, difficulty, and a small bonus for solutions that state a
  technique early. This favors examples whose proof strategy is
  explicit.

  </div>
- <span id="A2.I3.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A2.I3.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A2.I3.i4.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Default:</span> fetch 10 BM25
  candidates, rerank by lexical score and difficulty, and choose an
  adaptive number of examples based on how concentrated the top
  retrieval scores are.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">B.3 </span>TerminalBench-2 Harness

<div id="A2.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The discovered TerminalBench-2 harness builds on
Terminus-KIRA \[<a href="#bib.bib67" class="ltx_ref"
title="Terminus-kira: boosting frontier model performance on terminal-bench with minimal harness">24</a>\],
inheriting its native tool calling (replacing Terminus 2’s ICL-based
JSON parsing), 30KB output cap, and multi-perspective completion
checklist. The main modification discovered by Meta-Harness is
<span id="A2.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">environment
bootstrapping</span>: before the agent loop begins, the harness runs a
compound shell command to gather a snapshot of the sandbox environment
and injects it into the initial prompt. The proposer’s hypothesis,
recorded verbatim from the search log, was:

</div>

<div id="A2.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<div id="A2.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The snapshot includes: the working directory, a listing of
<span id="A2.SS3.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">/app</span> (truncated to 20
entries for large directories), available programming languages and
their versions (Python, GCC, G++, Node, Java, Rust, Go), installed
package managers (pip, apt-get), and available memory. This eliminates
the 2–4 exploratory turns that agents typically spend discovering what
tools and files are available, allowing the model to begin productive
work immediately. The bootstrapping command is guarded by a 15-second
timeout and fails silently, so it does not break the agent in unusual
environments. The full implementation adds roughly 80 lines on top of
Terminus-KIRA. <a href="#A2.F9" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Per-task analysis. ‣ B.3 TerminalBench-2 Harness ‣ Appendix B Discovered Harnesses ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">9</span></a> summarizes the harness
structure.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Per-task analysis.

<div id="A2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Compared to Terminus-KIRA, the discovered harness gains on 7 of 89
tasks, with the largest improvements on
<span id="A2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">protein-assembly</span> and
<span id="A2.SS3.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">path-tracing</span>. The gaining
tasks share a common property: they require domain-specific tooling
whose availability cannot be assumed in advance (bioinformatics
libraries, rendering pipelines, chess engines, cryptographic utilities,
CoreWars simulators). Without the bootstrap, the agent spends its first
2–4 turns probing the environment; on tasks with tight turn budgets or
where early wrong assumptions cascade, those wasted turns can be the
difference between pass and fail. This suggests that the bootstrap’s
value is largest when the environment is non-obvious, and the task
requires the agent to match its strategy to what is actually installed.

</div>

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure">Figure 9: </span><span
id="A2.F9.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Discovered TerminalBench-2
harness.</span> The harness inherits Terminus-KIRA’s native tool
calling, output cap, and completion checklist (green). The <span
id="A2.F9.4.2" class="ltx_text"
style="--ltx-bg-color:#FBE6E4;">environment bootstrap</span> (red) is
the component discovered by Meta-Harness: it gathers a sandbox snapshot
before the agent loop begins, eliminating early exploratory
turns.</figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Dataset Details

<div id="A3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.1 </span>OOD Text Classification Datasets

<div id="A3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

- <span id="A3.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i1.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">SciCite</span> is a 3-way
  citation-intent classification benchmark introduced by
  Cohan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib114" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Structural scaffolds for citation intent classification in scientific publications">13</a>\].
  Each example consists of a citation context from a scientific paper,
  labeled by the citation’s rhetorical role, such as background, method,
  or result. The task tests whether a model can infer why one paper
  cites another from the local scientific context.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i2.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">FiNER-139</span> is a financial numeric
  entity recognition benchmark introduced by
  Loukas<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib113" class="ltx_ref"
  title="FiNER: financial numeric entity recognition for xbrl tagging">28</a>\].
  It consists of word-level annotations from financial filings with 139
  fine-grained XBRL entity types, making it substantially more
  fine-grained than standard sentence-level classification tasks. The
  benchmark tests whether a model can identify and classify numeric
  financial entities from context.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Amazon
  Reviews</span> is the English portion of the Multilingual Amazon
  Reviews Corpus introduced by Keung<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
  et al.</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib112" class="ltx_ref"
  title="The multilingual amazon reviews corpus">21</a>\]. In our
  setting, it is used as a 5-way review rating prediction task, where
  the label corresponds to the review’s star rating. This benchmark
  evaluates general-domain sentiment and rating prediction from product
  review text.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Financial
  PhraseBank</span> is a 3-way financial sentiment benchmark introduced
  by Malo<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib111" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Good debt or bad debt: detecting semantic orientations in economic texts">31</a>\].
  It consists of sentences from financial news and related economic text
  labeled as positive, neutral, or negative with respect to market
  sentiment. The task evaluates domain-specific sentiment classification
  in finance.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i5.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">GoEmotions</span> is a fine-grained
  emotion classification benchmark introduced by
  Demszky<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib110" class="ltx_ref"
  title="GoEmotions: a dataset of fine-grained emotions">14</a>\]. It
  contains English Reddit comments annotated with 27 emotion categories
  plus a neutral category, and is commonly treated as a 28-way
  classification task. The benchmark tests nuanced affect recognition
  beyond coarse positive-negative sentiment.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i6"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i6.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i6.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Banking77</span> is a fine-grained
  intent classification benchmark introduced by
  Casanueva<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib109" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Efficient intent detection with dual sentence encoders">10</a>\].
  It contains online banking user utterances labeled with 77 intents,
  covering a wide range of customer service requests. The task evaluates
  single-domain intent detection with a large label space.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i7"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i7.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i7.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AG
  News</span> is a 4-way news topic classification benchmark commonly
  associated with the text classification setup of
  Zhang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
  \[<a href="#bib.bib108" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Character-level convolutional networks for text classification">59</a>\].
  Examples are labeled with broad news categories such as world, sports,
  business, and science/technology. It is a standard general-domain
  benchmark for topic classification.

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i8"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i8.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i8.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">SciTail</span> is a science-domain
  textual entailment benchmark in which the task is to predict whether a
  hypothesis is entailed by a premise sentence in a science-focused
  inference setting \[<a href="#bib.bib115" class="ltx_ref"
  title="SciTaiL: a textual entailment dataset from science question answering">23</a>\].

  </div>
- <span id="A3.I1.i9"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A3.I1.i9.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A3.I1.i9.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">TweetEval
  (Hate)</span> is the hate-speech subset of the TweetEval benchmark
  introduced by Barbieri<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
  al.</span> \[<a href="#bib.bib106" class="ltx_ref"
  title="TweetEval: unified benchmark and comparative evaluation for tweet classification">6</a>\].
  It is a binary tweet classification task for detecting hateful versus
  non-hateful content within a unified social-media evaluation suite.
  This benchmark tests robust classification in noisy, short-form social
  media text.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.2 </span>Math Retrieval Corpus

<div id="A3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

<a href="#A3.T10" class="ltx_ref"
title="In C.2 Math Retrieval Corpus ‣ Appendix C Dataset Details ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">10</span></a> lists the datasets composing
the retrieval corpus used in <a href="#S4.SS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="4.2 Harnesses for Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning ‣ 4 Experiments ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4.2</span></a>. The raw sources contain
more problems than the final corpus; several filtering steps were
applied before merging. NuminaMath-1.5 was filtered to competition-math
subsets (AMC/AIME, olympiad references, number theory, inequalities, and
related sources), discarding lower-quality web-scraped entries.
OpenMathReasoning was deduplicated to one solution per problem
(retaining the solution with the highest pass rate on an independent
verifier), and problems whose source matched any evaluation benchmark
family (IMO, AIME, HMMT, SMT, USAMO, Putnam) were removed before
deduplication. The entire corpus was then decontaminated against all
evaluation benchmarks and the search set used during harness search,
using exact prefix matching followed by fuzzy Jaccard similarity
(threshold 0.8); any corpus problem matching an eval problem under
either criterion was discarded. Solutions from OpenMathReasoning and
DeepMath are truncated to 5,000 characters to limit retrieval context
length. At runtime, the selected harness further restricts retrieval to
entries with non-empty solutions shorter than 4,000 characters.
Retrieved solutions are truncated again to 3,000 characters when
inserted into the prompt. For the geometry route, the harness also
constructs a separate hard-reference index from NuminaMath problems with
difficulty greater than 6.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.3 </span>Math IMO-level Test Set

<div id="A3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

The main text aggregates results over 200 IMO-level problems drawn from
IMO-AnswerBench, IMO-ProofBench, ArXivMath December 2025, and ArXivMath
January 2026. The 200-problem evaluation set consists of a stratified
100-problem subset of IMO-AnswerBench, together with all problems from
the other three benchmarks. This per-benchmark breakdown is useful
because the four datasets mix answer-style, proof, and research-style
problems, which are aggregated together in the main paper for brevity.
When included, the table in this section should report each benchmark
separately for both <span id="A3.SS3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Base</span> and
<span id="A3.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">Meta-Harness</span> across the five
held-out models.

</div>

<div id="A4" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix D </span>Practical Implementation Tips

<div id="A4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Meta-Harness is largely domain-agnostic: we expect it to apply in any
setting where a language model is wrapped by a task-specific harness.
Applying it in a new domain, however, requires operating in a relatively
new regime of LLM-assisted coding, where the proposer conditions on
long-horizon histories of prior runs and writes programs whose effects
may only become visible many steps later. In getting this workflow to
work reliably, we found a small set of practical choices that mattered
consistently across the three domains studied in this paper. The
guidelines below are not themselves scientific claims about the method;
they are engineering lessons from building and running the system, which
we hope will make it easier for future work to apply Meta-Harness in
other domains.

</div>

<div id="A4.p2" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

- <span id="A4.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Write a good
  skill.</span> The skill text is the primary interface for steering the
  search, and its quality is the strongest lever on whether the loop
  works. The proposer receives a natural-language
  skill \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
  title="Agentskills/agentskills">3</a>\] that defines its role, the
  directory layout, CLI commands, and output format. In practice, the
  skill should constrain outputs and safety-relevant behavior, not the
  proposer’s diagnosis procedure: it should specify what is forbidden,
  what artifacts to produce, and what objectives to optimize, while
  leaving the model free to inspect scores, traces, and prior code as
  needed. Our intuition from inspecting logs from Meta-Harness runs is
  that after enough iterations, the accumulated traces often shape the
  proposer’s behavior more than the skill itself. In our experience,
  iterating on the skill text had a larger effect on search quality than
  changing iteration count or population size. Expect to run a few short
  evolution runs (3–5 iterations each) specifically to debug and refine
  the skill before committing to a full run.

  </div>
- <span id="A4.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Start with a
  baseline harness and a search set that is hard for it.</span> Write a
  simple baseline (e.g., few-shot prompting), then construct the search
  set by either filtering for examples that the baseline gets wrong or
  selecting a diverse subset of difficult instances. The search has
  little to optimize if the baseline already saturates the evaluation.
  Keep the search set small enough for roughly 50 full evaluations per
  run (50–100 examples in our classification experiments, 88 problems
  for math retrieval); a fast, discriminative eval is more valuable than
  a large one.

  </div>
- <span id="A4.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Log
  everything in a format that is easy to navigate.</span> Evaluation
  code should write code, scores, and execution traces in a form that
  the proposer can query reliably. In practice, this means using
  machine-readable formats such as JSON, organizing artifacts
  hierarchically, choosing reasonable and consistent file names, and
  adopting naming schemes that make simple tools such as regex search
  work well.

  </div>
- <span id="A4.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Make logs
  queryable through a small CLI (optional, but helpful).</span> Each
  harness gets a directory containing source code, scores, and execution
  traces, but as the history grows, raw filesystem access alone becomes
  cumbersome. A short CLI that lists the Pareto frontier, shows
  top-$`k`$ harnesses, and diffs code and results between pairs of runs
  can make the experience store much easier to use, and querying such
  CLIs is closely aligned with the workflows on which coding agents are
  trained. If relevant offline experience exists (rollouts from other
  models, solved problem corpora, relevant papers), converting it into
  the same directory structure can also help warm-start exploration and
  ground new ideas. This layer helps the proposer save tokens it may
  have wasted on navigation.

  </div>
- <span id="A4.I1.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Lightweight
  validation before expensive benchmarks.</span> Write a small
  validation test that imports the module, instantiates the class, and
  calls both methods on a tiny set of examples. Harnesses proposed
  during the search should pass this test before being fully evaluated.
  A simple test script can catch most malformed or nonfunctional
  candidates in seconds and keep the cost of failures near zero.

  </div>
- <span id="A4.I1.i6"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A4.I1.i6.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

  <span id="A4.I1.i6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Automate
  evaluation outside the proposer.</span> Running evals is simple enough
  that it is not worth making the proposer do it. A separate harness
  should score candidates and write results to the filesystem.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix E </span>Extended Related Work

<div id="A5.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

This appendix expands the brief discussion in
<a href="#S2" class="ltx_ref"
title="2 Related Work ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> and situates Meta-Harness
relative to several neighboring lines of work that we could not cover in
detail in the main text. A recurring distinction is that Meta- Harness
optimizes executable harness implementations and provides the proposer
with selective access to prior code, scores, and execution traces via
the filesystem.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### AlphaEvolve / OpenEvolve.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

AlphaEvolve \[<a href="#bib.bib48" class="ltx_ref"
title="Alphaevolve: a coding agent for scientific and algorithmic discovery">34</a>\]
and OpenEvolve \[<a href="#bib.bib51" class="ltx_ref"
title="OpenEvolve: an open-source evolutionary coding agent">42</a>\]
evolve code via LLM-guided mutations with structured feedback: the
proposer receives a program database with scalar scores (4–22K tokens
per step; <a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>) and applies fixed mutation
strategies to tournament-selected parents. These methods are designed
for algorithm discovery and optimization (mathematical conjectures,
scheduling heuristics, hardware kernels), where the search target is a
single stateless function with a clean scalar objective, and mutations
are local. Harness engineering is a different regime: harnesses are
stateful programs that accumulate experience across many examples, and a
single design choice (e.g., what to store in memory) can cascade through
an entire evaluation sequence. Meta-Harness addresses this by giving an
unstructured coding agent full filesystem access, letting it selectively
read any prior candidate’s source code, execution traces, and scores.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### GEPA.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

GEPA \[<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gepa: reflective prompt evolution can outperform reinforcement learning">1</a>\]
is the closest text optimizer in terms of feedback richness, providing
rollout traces per candidate. It is designed for prompt optimization on
tasks with short feedback loops (math problems, instruction-following,
code optimization), where each rollout is a single LLM call or a short
pipeline. In this regime, per-candidate reflection works well: one
prompt, one answer, one score. Harness engineering requires reasoning
across many examples and many candidates simultaneously: understanding
why a retrieval strategy works for one class of problems but degrades on
another requires comparing execution traces across the full population.
GEPA operates on one candidate at a time (2–8K tokens per step;
<a href="#S1.T1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 1 Introduction ‣ Meta-Harness: End-to-End Optimization of Model Harnesses"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span> <span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>), with a fixed critique format
that must anticipate what information is relevant. Meta-Harness gives
the proposer access to <span id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_italic">all</span> prior candidates
simultaneously and lets the agent decide what to examine.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3" class="section ltx_paragraph">

#### Prompt orchestration frameworks.

<div id="A5.SS0.SSS0.Px3.p1" class="ltx_para ltx_noindent">

Several systems provide structured abstractions for composing
multi-stage LLM programs. LMQL \[<a href="#bib.bib103" class="ltx_ref"
title="Prompting is programming: a query language for large language models">7</a>\],
LangChain \[<a href="#bib.bib104" class="ltx_ref" title="LangChain">12</a>\],
and DSPy \[<a href="#bib.bib105" class="ltx_ref"
title="DSPy: compiling declarative language model calls into self-improving pipelines">22</a>\]
make prompt engineering more systematic by providing higher-level
interfaces for prompt templates, control flow, and modular LLM
pipelines. These frameworks help developers specify and organize LLM
programs, but they still typically require manual design of retrieval
policies, memory updates, and orchestration logic. Meta-Harness operates
at a different level: it searches over the *implementation* of these
policies in executable code, treating the harness itself as the
optimization target.

</div>

</div>

</div>

content="Meta-Harness: End-To-End Optimization of Model Harnesses">

</div>
